The '''2018 United States House of Representatives elections''' were held on November 6, 2018. The 2018 House elections were [[2018 United States elections|midterm elections]] that occurred during President [[Donald Trump]]'s first term in office. Early voting took place in some states in the weeks preceding Election Day. Voters chose representatives from all 435 [[List of United States congressional districts|congressional districts]] across each of the 50 [[U.S. state]]s to serve in the [[116th United States Congress]]. 

Led by House Minority Leader [[Nancy Pelosi]], the [[Democratic Party (United States)|Democratic Party]] won control of the House of Representatives in the 2018 elections. The Democrats gained a net total of 41 seats. The 41-seat gain was the Democrats' largest gain of House seats since the post-[[Watergate]] [[1974 United States House of Representatives elections|1974 elections]], when they picked up 49 seats. Democrats also won the popular vote by an 8.6% margin, the largest margin of victory on record for a minority party.

== Results summary and analysis ==
The [[Democratic Party (United States)|Democratic Party]] won control of the House of Representatives in the 2018 midterm elections. The Democrats gained a net total of 41 seats from the total number of seats they had won in the [[2016 United States House of Representatives elections|2016 elections]]. This was their largest gain of House seats in an election since the [[1974 United States House of Representatives elections|1974 elections]], when the Democrats gained 49 House seats.<ref>Multiple sources:
*{{cite web | url=https://www.latimes.com/politics/la-me-pol-valadao-cox-victory-20181128-story.html | title=TJ Cox beats Republican Rep. David Valadao to give Democrats gain of 40 House seats, seven in California | first=Mark Z. | last=Barabak | website=[[Los Angeles Times]]| date=November 28, 2018 }}
*{{cite news | url=https://www.npr.org/2018/11/14/667818539/it-was-a-big-blue-wave-democrats-pick-up-most-house-seats-in-a-generation | title=It Was A Big, Blue Wave: Democrats Pick Up Most House Seats In A Generation | publisher=[[NPR]] | last=Montanaro | first=Domenico | date=November 14, 2018 | access-date=November 18, 2018}}
*{{cite web | url=https://www.nbcnews.com/politics/first-read/democrats-had-good-showing-election-day-it-s-been-even-n935126 | title=Democrats had a good showing on Election Day. It's been even better for them since. | first1=Chuck | last1=Todd | first2=Mark | last2=Murray | first3=Carrie | last3=Dann | publisher=[[NBC News]] | date=November 12, 2018 | access-date=November 14, 2018}}
*{{cite news | url=https://www.independent.co.uk/news/world/americas/us-politics/midterms-2018/midterms-results-democrats-blue-wave-late-vote-counts-us-election-trump-republican-a8631176.html |archive-url=https://ghostarchive.org/archive/20220501/https://www.independent.co.uk/news/world/americas/us-politics/midterms-2018/midterms-results-democrats-blue-wave-late-vote-counts-us-election-trump-republican-a8631176.html |archive-date=May 1, 2022 |url-access=subscription |url-status=live | title=Midterms: Late results reveal Democrats 'blue wave' as party secures best election performance since 1974 | first=Chris | last=Baynes | work=[[The Independent]]| date=November 13, 2018 | access-date=November 14, 2018}}{{cbignore}}
</ref> Democrats won the popular vote by more than 9.7 million votes, or 8.6%,<ref>{{cite web | url=https://docs.google.com/spreadsheets/d/1WxDaxD5az6kdOjJncmGph37z0BPNhV1fNAH_g7IkpC0/edit#gid=0 | title=2018 House Popular Vote Tracker | last1=Wasserman | first1=Dave | last2=Flinn | first2=Ally | publisher=[[The Cook Political Report with Amy Walter|The Cook Political Report]] | access-date=January 2, 2019 | archive-date=January 3, 2019 | archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20190103034238/https://docs.google.com/spreadsheets/d/1WxDaxD5az6kdOjJncmGph37z0BPNhV1fNAH_g7IkpC0/edit#gid=0 | url-status=dead }}</ref> the largest margin of victory on record for a minority party.<ref>{{cite news | url=https://www.cnn.com/2018/12/06/politics/latest-house-vote-blue-wave/index.html | title=Latest House results confirm 2018 wasn't a blue wave. It was a blue tsunami. | last=Enten | first=Harry | date=December 6, 2018 | website=[[CNN]]}}</ref>

Prior to the 2018 elections, the [[Republican Party (United States)|Republican Party]] had held the House majority since [[2010 United States House of Representatives elections|January 2011]].<ref>{{Cite web | url=https://www.nbcnews.com/politics/congress/what-happens-now-democrats-will-retake-house-n930556 | title=What happens now that Democrats will retake the House | website=[[NBC News]] | date=November 7, 2018 | language=en | access-date=February 27, 2019}}</ref> According to the [[Associated Press]]' statistical analysis, [[gerrymandering]] may have cost the Democrats 16 seats in the 2018 House elections.<ref>{{cite web | url=https://www.apnews.com/9fd72a4c1c5742aead977ee27815d776 | first= David A. | last=Lieb | website=apnews.com | date=March 21, 2019 | title=AP: GOP won more seats in 2018 than suggested by vote share}}</ref> Voter turnout in these elections was 50.3%,<ref name="2018 voter turnout">{{cite web | url=http://www.electproject.org/2018g | title=2018g – United States Elections Project | website=electproject.org | access-date=December 11, 2018}}</ref> the highest turnout in a U.S. midterm election since [[1914 United States House of Representatives elections|1914]].<ref>{{cite web | url=http://www.electproject.org/national-1789-present | title=National General Election VEP Turnout Rates, 1789–Present | website=electproject.org | access-date=December 11, 2018}}</ref>

The House Republicans' passage of the widely unpopular [[American Health Care Act of 2017]] to repeal the [[Affordable Care Act]], as well as opposition to [[Political positions of Donald Trump|Trump's policies]], his low approval ratings, and questions about his personal stamina for office, are credited for the Democratic takeover of the House.<ref>{{Cite web |url=https://www.niskanencenter.org/how-republicans-lost-2018-by-being-too-close-to-trump/ |title=How Republicans Lost 2018 by Being Too Close to Trump |date=June 17, 2020 |website=Niskanen Center |access-date=April 30, 2025}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web |url=https://www.vox.com/policy-and-politics/2018/11/7/18070152/midterm-elections-2018-results-trump-obamacare-repeal |title=Trump's biggest midterm blunder: embracing Obamacare repeal |first=Dylan |last=Scott |website=[[Vox (website)|Vox]] |date=November 7, 2018 |access-date=April 30, 2025}}</ref>

(Note that the results summary does not include blank and over/under votes which were included in the official results or votes cast in the [[2018 North Carolina's 9th congressional district election|voided election]] in [[North Carolina's 9th congressional district]].)

===Veteran candidate recruitment===
The Democratic Congressional Campaign Committee actively recruited military veterans and candidates with national security backgrounds for competitive districts in 2018.<ref>{{cite web|url=https://www.npr.org/2017/09/08/549172533/with-an-eye-on-the-house-democrats-turn-to-veterans-for-2018-races|title=With An Eye On The House, Democrats Turn To Veterans For 2018 Races|publisher=NPR|date=September 8, 2017}}</ref> Political analysts noted that candidates with military experience could appeal to moderate and independent voters in swing districts.<ref>{{cite web|url=https://abcnews.go.com/Politics/democrats-back-military-veterans-candidates-battle-retake-house/story?id=51088993|title=Democrats back military veterans as candidates in their battle to retake the House|publisher=ABC News|date=November 7, 2017}}</ref> Of the 24 Republicans unseated by Democrats, eight were defeated by veterans or former national security officials, including Jason Crow (Colorado), Elissa Slotkin (Michigan), Mikie Sherrill (New Jersey), Chrissy Houlahan (Pennsylvania), and Elaine Luria (Virginia).<ref>{{cite web|url=https://theintercept.com/2018/11/08/military-veteran-candidates-midterms/|title=Mixed Results for Military Veterans as Democratic Candidates|publisher=The Intercept|date=November 8, 2018}}</ref><ref>{{cite web|url=https://alphademocrat.com/2018-blue-wave-veteran-democrats-house/|title=2018 Blue Wave: How Veteran Democrats Helped Flip the House|publisher=Alpha Democrat Research}}</ref>

== Retirements ==
[[File:US House 2018 retirements.svg|thumb|350px|Retiring incumbents by district<br />{{legend0|#92C5DE|Democratic incumbent ran}}<br />{{legend0|#0671B0|Democratic incumbent retired or lost renomination}}<br />{{legend0|#F48882|Republican incumbent ran}}<br />{{legend0|#CA0120|Republican incumbent retired or lost renomination}}<br />{{legend0|#BB91B1|Democratic and Republican incumbent ran}}<br />{{legend0|#6D6D6D|Vacant or new district}}]]
In the November general elections, 55 incumbents did not seek re-election. This election cycle saw the third most retirements in an election cycle in history behind [[1992 United States House of Representatives elections|1992]] and [[2026 United States House of Representatives elections|2026]].<ref>{{Cite web |title=House retirements tied for most this century |url=https://www.axios.com/2026/03/02/members-of-congress-retirements-record-high |access-date=March 9, 2026 |website=Axios}}</ref>

== Vacancies during the 115th Congress that did not result in special elections ==
Three Republicans, all of whom had already announced their retirement, resigned early.

# [[Florida's 6th congressional district|Florida 6]]: [[Ron DeSantis]] resigned on September 10, 2018, retroactive to September 1, to focus on his campaign for Governor of Florida.<ref>{{Cite web |date=2018-09-10 |title=Amid heated governor's race, Ron DeSantis resigns from Congress |url=https://www.nbcnews.com/politics/congress/rep-ron-desantis-resigns-congress-n908146 |access-date=2024-05-07 |website=NBC News |language=en}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web |title=Florida GOP nominee for governor resigns from Congress |url=https://www.jacksonville.com/story/news/politics/elections/local/2018/09/10/florida-gop-nominee-for-governor-ron-desantis-resigns-from-congress/10799671007/ |access-date=2024-05-23 |website=The Florida Times-Union |language=en-US}}</ref> This seat was then left vacant until the 116th Congress was sworn in on January 3, 2019.
# [[Oklahoma's 1st congressional district|Oklahoma 1]]: [[Jim Bridenstine]] resigned on April 23, 2018, to become head of NASA.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Foust |first=Jeff |date=2018-04-23 |title=Bridenstine sworn in as NASA administrator |url=https://spacenews.com/bridenstine-sworn-in-as-nasa-administrator/ |access-date=2024-05-07 |website=SpaceNews |language=en-US}}</ref>  This seat was then left vacant until [[Kevin Hern]] was sworn in early on November 13, 2018, having won the regularly scheduled November 6, 2018 election for the next full term.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Media |first=Griffin |title=First District Congressman Kevin Hern Sworn Into Office |url=https://www.news9.com/story/5e35d3d42f69d76f62018200/first-district-congressman-kevin-hern-sworn-into-office |access-date=2024-05-07 |website=www.news9.com |language=en}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web |last=Media |first=Griffin |title=First District Congressman-Elect Kevin Hern To Be Sworn In Early |url=https://www.news9.com/story/5e35d4332f69d76f620184ca/first-district-congressmanelect-kevin-hern-to-be-sworn-in-early |access-date=2024-05-07 |website=www.news9.com |language=en}}</ref>
# {{ushr|WV|3|X}}: [[Evan Jenkins (politician)|Evan Jenkins]] announced his retirement May 8, 2017, to [[2018 United States Senate election in West Virginia|run for U.S. Senator]].<ref name="AtlanticList" /> He lost the nomination and then resigned September 30, 2018, when appointed to the [[Supreme Court of Appeals of West Virginia]]. This seat was then left vacant until the 116th Congress was sworn in on January 3, 2019.

== Maine ==
{{Main|2018 United States House of Representatives elections in Maine}}

{{See also|List of United States representatives from Maine}}

The 1–1 tie became a 2–0 Democratic hold. This was the first use of [[Instant-runoff voting|ranked choice voting]] to decide a House race.

== North Carolina ==
{{Main|2018 United States House of Representatives elections in North Carolina}}

{{See also|List of United States representatives from North Carolina}}

Due to allegations of electoral fraud, the 116th Congress was sworn in with one seat vacant.<ref>{{Cite news | url=https://www.nbcnews.com/politics/politics-news/fraud-allegations-leave-north-carolina-house-race-unresolved-no-clear-n942816 | title=Why is North Carolina's 9th congressional district race still undecided? | work=NBC News | access-date=December 4, 2018}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web | url=https://www.carolinajournal.com/news-article/cooper-ditches-call-for-interim-elections-board-9th-district-hearing-postponed | title=Cooper ditches call for interim elections board; Harris campaign to sue for certification | access-date=February 27, 2019 | work=[[Carolina Journal]] | first=Dan | last=Way | date=January 2019}}</ref> On February 21, 2019, a new election was ordered by the state election board.<ref name="Re-run">{{cite news | title=North Carolina orders new 9th District election, throws out Harris-McCready results | url=https://www.washingtonpost.com/politics/candidate-says-new-congressional-election-warranted-in-north-carolina/2019/02/21/acae4482-35e0-11e9-854a-7a14d7fec96a_story.html | newspaper=[[The Washington Post]] | date=February 21, 2019 | first=Amy | last=Gardner}}</ref>

=== Puerto Rico ===
{{Main|List of Resident Commissioners of Puerto Rico}}

The [[Resident Commissioner of Puerto Rico]] is not up for re-election until 2020.<ref name=pasquines/> Currently held by [[Republican Party of Puerto Rico (1903)|Republican]] [[Jenniffer González]], who was first elected in 2016, the Resident Commissioner is the only member of the United States House of Representatives to serve a four-year term.<ref name=pasquines/>
